Crimson Dynasty — Chapters Thirty-Eight & Thirty-Nine

Chapter Thirty-Eight

The Red Passage

The first rule of boarding a slave ship was apparently not to look like someone about to board a slave ship.

Caldrin informed them of this while painting dirt across Zee’s face.

Zee recoiled.

“What are you doing?”

“Improving you.”

“You made me dirty.”

“You were already dirty.”

“That was travel dirt.”

Caldrin stared.

“I apologize for disrespecting the distinction.”

Rez stood nearby in plain clothes, his smaller sword hidden beneath a dark coat. Ner wore gloves over his hands, every trace of Pure Golden Mana buried.

Aurelia had changed too.

No royal clothing.

No Darkheart symbols.

No visible witch crown.

Her hair was tied back, her Dragon nature buried so deeply Zee could barely feel its presence even knowing what to look for.

Theron remained beneath the ridge with Vale.

He hated that arrangement.

Everyone knew.

“I can walk.”

Vale folded his arms.

“You nearly fell mounting the horse.”

“I had been imprisoned for six years.”

“Yes.”

“So perhaps sympathy.”

“No.”

Theron looked toward Aurelia.

“She always chooses terrible people.”

Aurelia did not even turn.

“Victor was enough. I learned.”

Zee winced.

Rez whispered, “That was brutal.”

Ner nodded approvingly.

Above them, Zareth circled behind thick clouds.

The Dragon Link remained tense.

Boats. People. Blood.

Zee closed his eyes.

“Which ship?”

An image came.

The center vessel.

Lower deck.

Four distinct presences surrounded by many others.

Not enough for Zareth to identify blood.

Enough to know where people were confined.

Zee opened his eyes.

“Center.”

Mara nodded.

“Khell’s vessel.”

Aurelia looked toward her.

“You are certain?”

“The center ship always carries the broker.”

“Always?”

Mara corrected herself immediately.

“It did when I worked these routes.”

Aurelia nodded.

Better.

Truth precise enough to trust.

The three black ships drifted slowly down the Marrow River.

Red lanterns hung along their decks.

Small boats ferried wealthy buyers toward them.

No kingdom flags.

No royal insignia.

That was the point.

No crown could be blamed for what happened on water nobody claimed.

Zee watched men and women climb onto the center vessel.

Witches.

Vampires.

Humans.

One wolf-blooded noble.

Everyone dressed too well.

Everyone pretending this was business.

His fangs sharpened.

Rez touched his shoulder.

“Control.”

“I know.”

“Good.”

“You say that every time.”

“Because you keep needing it.”

Ner looked toward Rez.

“You sound seventy.”

Rez glared.

“I lived seventeen years.”

“Historically.”

Rez took one slow breath.

“I am saving all of this.”

“For when?”

“When I am taller again.”

Ner smiled.

“That assumes you grow faster than me.”

Zee laughed.

Aurelia looked at them.

“Are we finished?”

All three answered at once.

“Yes.”

Zareth sent amusement through the Link.

Aurelia sighed.

Then pointed toward the river.

“We go in two groups.”

“Caldrin, Mara, Rez.”

Rez nodded.

“You board through the buyer entrance.”

Zee frowned.

“Why Rez?”

“Because no one there expects Crown Prince Rez Vesper to look ten.”

Rez smiled.

Finally.

Aurelia continued.

“Zee, Ner, and I use the maintenance skiff beneath the stern.”

Ner looked toward her.

“And Zareth?”

Aurelia glanced upward.

“He waits.”

The Dragon Link exploded with rejection.

No.

Zee looked up.

“He says no.”

“I assumed.”

“He says he can help.”

“He can help by not setting the ship on fire while prisoners are below deck.”

A pause.

Then:

Small fire.

Zee covered his mouth.

Aurelia narrowed her eyes.

“What?”

“Nothing.”

“Zee.”

“He said small fire.”

Rez laughed first.

Aurelia looked toward the sky.

“Tell him if he burns that ship before I give the signal, I will personally send him back to his mother.”

The Link went silent.

Zee smiled.

“He understands.”

“Good.”

They moved.

⸻

Rez boarded the Red Passage as a servant.

He hated that less than being treated like an actual child.

Caldrin played the role of minor merchant.

Mara acted as broker.

That part required very little pretending.

The guard at the boarding ramp barely looked at Rez.

“Boy carries?”

Caldrin handed him a small case.

Rez accepted it with exaggerated effort.

Caldrin nearly smiled.

The guard waved them through.

The moment they crossed onto the deck, Rez changed.

Not visibly.

Inside.

Seventeen years of training settled behind ten-year-old eyes.

He counted guards.

Doors.

Stairs.

Weapons.

Possible exits.

Two vampires on deck.

Three witches.

Six humans.

One buyer carrying silver restraints.

One servant watching too carefully.

Mara whispered without moving her lips.

“Khell’s people use red cord on the wrist.”

Rez glanced.

Three guards.

Two servants.

One man near the stern.

Red cord.

Good.

Caldrin murmured, “Do not stare.”

“I am not.”

“You stare professionally.”

“That is not a thing.”

“It is now.”

The auction bell rang again.

A buyer laughed nearby.

“Veyrath, they say.”

Another answered.

“Real?”

“Supposedly.”

Rez’s jaw tightened.

“Dragon blood?”

“Better.”

“Dragon mixed with witch.”

Rez looked toward the river instead.

If he turned now, he would hit someone.

Caldrin noticed.

“Later.”

Rez nodded.

The buyers moved toward the lower auction chamber.

Rez followed.

⸻

Beneath the stern, Zee discovered that infiltration through a maintenance hatch was far less impressive than stories made it sound.

The hatch smelled terrible.

Ner climbed through first.

He immediately looked back.

“I hate this plan.”

Zee whispered, “You agreed.”

“I was younger then.”

“That was ten minutes ago.”

“A lot has happened.”

Aurelia entered last.

The maintenance corridor was narrow enough that Zee’s horns scraped the overhead beams twice.

He stopped.

“This place was designed badly.”

Ner looked back.

“It was designed for people without horns.”

“Exactly.”

Aurelia pushed both forward.

They descended.

Zee listened.

Heartbeats above.

Below.

Ahead.

Many.

Then four weak rhythms behind iron.

“Here.”

Aurelia stopped.

“Certain?”

“Yes.”

The corridor ended at a locked service door.

Ner removed one glove.

Pure Golden Mana gathered around his fingertips.

Zee looked at him.

“Careful.”

Ner raised an eyebrow.

“You are telling me that?”

“Yes.”

“That is alarming.”

Ner touched the lock.

Golden Mana moved through it.

Not forcing.

Reading.

The mechanism opened.

No alarm.

Ner smiled.

Zee frowned.

“You are pleased with yourself.”

“Yes.”

“Annoying.”

“You would be worse.”

True.

The next chamber contained cages.

Not cells.

Cages.

Seven spaces arranged around a central platform.

Three empty.

Four occupied.

Aurelia stopped at the entrance.

Zee had seen his mother angry.

Terrified.

Heartbroken.

He had never seen her look like this.

The four captives were alive.

Barely.

An old man lay curled inside the first cage.

A woman with silver-red hair occupied the second.

A younger Dragon-blooded man sat shackled in the third.

And in the fourth…

A woman lifted her head.

Dark red eyes.

Long hair nearly white from stress and age that Zee could not guess.

Thin.

Scarred.

Alive.

Aurelia whispered:

“Seraphine.”

The woman stared.

Nothing happened.

No recognition.

Then Aurelia stepped closer.

“Seraphine.”

Her voice broke.

The captive’s eyes widened.

She stood too quickly and stumbled against the bars.

“No.”

Aurelia reached the cage.

“It is me.”

“No.”

“Seraphine.”

“No.”

Tears appeared instantly.

“You are dead.”

Aurelia wrapped both hands around the bars.

“So are you.”

Seraphine laughed once.

Then sobbed.

“Aurelia?”

“Yes.”

The word barely left Aurelia before Seraphine reached through the bars.

Aurelia caught her hands.

For several breaths, Zee forgot where they were.

Forgot the auction above them.

Forgot Khell.

Forgot guards.

He watched his mother become someone’s sister.

Not queen.

Not survivor.

Not Dragon-Witch.

Little flame.

Seraphine pressed her forehead against the iron.

“You got old.”

Aurelia laughed through tears.

“You too.”

“I hate you.”

“I know.”

“You left.”

“You told me to run.”

“I was eight!”

“So was I!”

Seraphine stared.

Then laughed again.

It hurt to hear.

Zee looked toward Ner.

Ner’s eyes had gone wet.

He immediately looked away.

Zee did not tease him.

Not this time.

Then Seraphine noticed Zee.

Her face changed.

She stared at the horns first.

Then the Crimson scales.

Then Aurelia.

“Aurelia…”

“My son.”

Seraphine looked back at Zee.

Zee lifted one hand awkwardly.

“Hi.”

Seraphine stared.

Ner whispered, “Powerful introduction.”

Zee elbowed him.

Seraphine laughed weakly.

That seemed to decide something.

“He looks like trouble.”

Aurelia wiped tears from her face.

“He is.”

Zee frowned.

“Mother.”

Seraphine looked at him again.

“What is your name?”

“Zee.”

Her expression softened.

“I am Seraphine.”

“I know.”

“Apparently.”

He stepped closer.

“I came to get you out.”

Aurelia looked at him.

“We came.”

Zee nodded.

“We.”

Better.

Seraphine looked between mother and son.

Then her face hardened.

“Khell.”

Aurelia’s grief vanished.

“Where?”

“Above.”

“Does he know we are here?”

“No.”

A voice came from the doorway.

“Yes.”

Everyone turned.

Damaris Khell stood there.

He did not look like a blood broker.

That annoyed Zee.

He looked ordinary.

Middle-aged.

Expensive coat.

Dark beard.

No visible supernatural traits.

No theatrical weapon.

Just a man who had built a fortune by turning people into inventory.

Six guards stood behind him.

Khell looked at Aurelia.

Then Zee.

Then Ner’s uncovered hand.

His expression became very still.

“Well.”

Zee hated that word.

Khell smiled.

“Tonight has become valuable.”

Aurelia moved in front of the cages.

“You have exactly one chance.”

“To?”

“Open them.”

Khell looked toward Seraphine.

“I spent years assembling this collection.”

“They are people.”

“To you.”

Ner’s Golden Mana ignited.

“To everyone.”

Khell looked at him.

Interest sharpened.

“Pure.”

Zee stepped in front of Ner.

“No.”

Khell’s eyes moved toward him.

Crimson.

Horns.

Scales.

Fangs visible now.

He frowned.

“I know you.”

Zee’s expression hardened.

“No, you do not.”

“The Darkheart child.”

“That is not knowing me.”

Khell smiled.

“Fair.”

Then he lifted one finger.

Every cage ignited with red runes.

The captives screamed.

Aurelia turned.

Khell backed into the corridor.

“One step and the restraints constrict.”

Ner’s face tightened.

“Again?”

Zee looked toward him.

“We are getting good at these.”

“This is not the time.”

“I know.”

Khell smiled.

“I assume Morcant failed.”

Aurelia stared.

“She is alive.”

His smile vanished.

Interesting.

Caldrin’s voice sounded from behind Khell.

“And disappointed.”

Khell turned.

Too late.

Caldrin’s blade touched his back.

Rez stood behind him.

Mara beside Rez.

The guards spun.

Blue-White Mana exploded.

Rez changed the corridor in one move.

Two guards were thrown into the wall.

A third lost footing as Blue-White frost spread beneath him.

Caldrin disarmed a fourth.

Mara drew a short blade and held it against Khell’s ribs.

Khell stared at her.

“You.”

Mara looked at him.

“Me.”

“You were paid.”

“I was.”

“You profited.”

“I did.”

“You do not get to become righteous because guilt became inconvenient.”

Mara’s face hardened.

“No.”

She pressed the knife harder.

“But I can become useful.”

Khell smiled.

Then vanished.

Not magic.

Trapdoor.

The floor beneath him dropped.

Caldrin swore.

Mara nearly fell after him.

Rez grabbed her.

Zee heard Khell landing below.

Alive.

Running.

Aurelia turned toward the cages.

“Let him go.”

Zee stared.

“What?”

“For now.”

“He has—”

“Our family is here.”

That decided it.

Ner moved toward the runes.

“I can break them.”

Seraphine grabbed the bars.

“No.”

Ner stopped.

“Why?”

“They are linked.”

“Everything here is linked.”

Seraphine looked toward Aurelia.

“Khell built failure into every cage.”

Aurelia’s face hardened.

“So we do not break them.”

Zee looked at the floor.

Then the walls.

Then smiled.

Ner immediately understood.

“Oh no.”

“What?”

“You have the wall idea again.”

“It worked.”

Aurelia stared at both boys.

“Explain.”

Zee pointed around the cages.

“We take the room.”

Rez blinked.

“What?”

“The cages are linked to the ship.”

“So?”

Zee smiled.

“Zareth is outside.”

Aurelia’s face changed instantly.

“No.”

“Mother.”

“No.”

“We do not burn anything.”

“No.”

“We remove the roof.”

“No.”

Rez looked toward Ner.

“This is going to happen.”

Ner nodded.

“It always happens.”

Zee sent one thought through the Dragon Link.

Careful.

Zareth answered.

Small fire?

“No fire.”

Disappointment.

Then:

Break.

Zee smiled.

Aurelia saw.

“Zee Darkheart—”

The ship shook.

Everyone looked up.

A massive impact struck the upper deck.

Buyers screamed.

Wood splintered.

Zareth roared.

The cages remained intact.

The ceiling did not.

A crimson Dragon claw tore through the roof.

Rez looked upward.

“He actually listened.”

Zee grinned.

“Mostly.”

Aurelia covered her face.

⸻

Chapter Thirty-Nine

The Family Khell Could Not Sell

The Red Passage stopped being an auction within thirty seconds.

It became evacuation.

Buyers fled toward the lifeboats.

Guards abandoned posts.

Someone screamed that Drakonis had declared war.

Someone else screamed that Zee Darkheart was attacking.

Neither was true.

No one waited for corrections.

Zareth tore open the upper deck with careful violence.

Careful for a Dragon still meant half the roof disappeared into the river.

The Dragon Link carried intense concentration.

No fire. Breaking only. Good?

Zee looked upward.

“Good!”

Aurelia stared at him.

“You are encouraging him.”

“He followed instructions!”

The entire vessel tilted.

Aurelia grabbed Zee before he slid into a cage.

“Barely!”

Rez and Caldrin fought the remaining guards near the corridor.

Rez’s smaller body moved differently now.

Five days earlier he would have hated that.

Tonight he used it.

A guard swung high, expecting a teenager’s reach.

Rez went under him.

Blue-White Mana flashed around the man’s ankle.

He fell.

Rez stepped onto the weapon hand.

“You really should stop looking at me like I am ten.”

The guard stared.

Rez paused.

“Physically.”

He kicked the sword away.

Caldrin smiled.

“Needs work.”

“I am busy!”

Ner stood before the cage runes.

Golden Mana spread across the floor.

Not touching the locks.

Reading the connections.

“Zee!”

“What?”

“They go into the hull.”

Zee looked.

Ner pointed.

“The cages are tied to the ship structure.”

Aurelia understood.

“If the ship breaks apart—”

“The restraints tighten.”

Zee looked toward the ceiling.

“Zareth!”

The Dragon stopped instantly.

Wood creaked.

The ship settled.

Zee sent:

Stop breaking.

Zareth answered with deep confusion.

You said break.

“I changed my mind.”

The Link carried outrage.

Rez shouted from the corridor.

“This is why battle plans exist!”

Zee ignored him.

Ner’s Golden Mana moved deeper.

“There is one central seal.”

“Where?”

“Below us.”

Where Khell had escaped.

Of course.

Aurelia looked toward the four captives.

Then Zee.

“No.”

Zee stared.

“I did not say anything.”

“You are going after him.”

“Yes.”

“No.”

“We need the seal.”

“Caldrin goes.”

Caldrin looked toward the corridor.

“Gladly.”

Zee shook his head.

“Ner can find it.”

Ner looked at Aurelia.

“I can.”

Aurelia’s expression hardened.

Ner continued.

“And Zee can sense Khell.”

Zee nodded.

“Heartbeat.”

Rez walked back into the chamber.

“I go too.”

Aurelia looked at his bloodied sleeve.

“You are injured.”

“Scratch.”

“Your body is ten.”

“My memory is still seventeen.”

“Your blood is currently coming out of you.”

Rez looked down.

“Minor complication.”

Seraphine gripped the bars.

“Aurelia.”

Everyone turned.

“Go.”

Aurelia stared.

“You just found me.”

“And I spent years waiting for someone to choose rescue over revenge.”

Seraphine looked toward the floor.

“If that seal controls these cages, go.”

Aurelia closed her eyes.

Then opened them.

“Vale stays here.”

He nodded.

“Zareth stays above and keeps the ship stable.”

The Dragon Link delivered a feeling of intense dissatisfaction.

“Tell him.”

Zee did.

Zareth answered:

Boring.

Zee almost smiled.

Aurelia looked at Zee, Ner, Rez, and Caldrin.

“Together.”

No argument.

They descended.

⸻

Khell’s lower chamber was beneath the waterline.

The air smelled of river mud, ink, blood, and magic.

Ner’s Golden Mana led them through two narrow corridors.

Zee listened.

Khell’s heartbeat ahead.

Fast.

Another heartbeat.

Then another.

“Two guards.”

Rez drew his sword.

His smaller hand fit the new hilt properly now.

“Finally.”

Aurelia looked at him.

“That enthusiasm worries me.”

“It should.”

They turned the corner.

The first guard attacked.

Rez took him.

Blue-White Mana shifted the man’s footing.

Caldrin disarmed the second.

Aurelia hit both with binding magic.

Ten seconds.

Done.

Zee looked impressed.

Rez smiled.

“Experience.”

Ner added, “And short legs.”

Rez glared.

Khell stood inside the final chamber.

Behind him was a metal pillar covered in red runes.

The central seal.

He held a knife.

Not against himself.

Against a boy.

One of the missing Veyrath captives.

Zee froze.

The boy looked perhaps fourteen.

Thin.

Dark red scales covered one cheek.

Khell held the blade beneath his jaw.

“Stop.”

Aurelia did.

Ner’s Golden Mana dimmed.

Rez lowered his sword slightly.

Caldrin did not.

Khell looked toward Aurelia.

“You destroyed years of work.”

“You purchased people.”

“I preserved bloodlines everyone else wanted extinct.”

Aurelia’s face became cold.

“In cages.”

“Alive.”

The captive boy whispered, “Do not listen.”

Khell tightened the knife.

Blood appeared.

Zee heard the boy’s heartbeat accelerate.

His horns heated.

Rez saw.

“Control.”

Zee breathed.

Khell’s gaze moved toward him.

“You are the one, then.”

Zee frowned.

“The one what?”

“The rumors.”

“Natural Witch.”

“Vampire.”

“Wolf.”

His eyes moved toward Zee’s horns.

“And something else.”

Zee did not answer.

Khell smiled.

“I have spent my life buying rare blood.”

“You are the first thing I have ever seen whose price I cannot calculate.”

Zee’s eyes narrowed.

“I am not for sale.”

“Everything is.”

Ner’s Golden Mana flashed.

“No.”

Khell looked toward him.

Then laughed softly.

“Two impossibilities in the same room.”

Rez stepped sideways.

Khell moved the knife.

“Do not.”

Rez stopped.

Aurelia’s voice remained calm.

“What do you want?”

“A way out.”

“No.”

“Then the boy dies.”

Zee’s face changed.

Khell looked at him.

“There.”

“That is the part of you people should fear.”

Zee stared.

“What?”

“You care.”

Khell smiled.

“That can always be priced.”

Zee’s Mana surged.

Aurelia looked sharply at him.

“Zee.”

He stopped himself.

Barely.

Khell saw.

Smiled wider.

He had found leverage.

Or thought he had.

The captive boy looked at Zee.

Then at Ner.

Then at Rez.

His eyes shifted toward the metal pillar.

Small.

Quick.

Ner saw.

The boy was telling them something.

Not with magic.

With his eyes.

Pillar.

Lower rune.

Ner followed.

One red symbol pulsed out of sequence.

He understood.

The boy had been watching Khell use the seal.

Ner slowly lowered his hands.

Khell relaxed slightly.

Mistake.

Rez moved first.

Not toward Khell.

Toward the ceiling.

Blue-White Mana struck a pipe above him.

It burst.

River water exploded downward.

Khell flinched.

The blade moved one inch away from the boy’s throat.

Enough.

Zee crossed the distance.

Vampire speed.

One hand caught Khell’s wrist.

The other grabbed the boy’s coat.

Zee pulled them apart.

Khell slashed.

The knife cut across Zee’s forearm.

Blood.

Pain.

Crimson scales spread around the wound.

Zee did not stop.

He threw Khell into the wall.

Caldrin pinned him before he could rise.

Aurelia caught the Veyrath boy.

Ner ran toward the pillar.

“Lower rune!”

The rescued boy shouted, “Twist it!”

Ner grabbed the rune with Golden Mana.

It resisted.

He frowned.

“This is not a lock.”

Aurelia looked over.

“What?”

“It is a contract.”

Khell laughed from the floor.

“Exactly.”

Blood contract.

Not Zee’s blood.

Khell’s.

Ner’s expression hardened.

“Then we need him.”

Caldrin smiled.

“Excellent.”

Khell stopped laughing.

Rez walked over.

“Open it.”

“No.”

Caldrin adjusted the knife against his shoulder.

Khell smiled through pain.

“You will not kill me while those cages depend on me.”

Aurelia looked toward the Veyrath boy.

“Does he need to be alive?”

The boy nodded.

“Yes.”

Khell smiled.

Aurelia continued.

“Conscious?”

The boy hesitated.

“I do not know.”

Khell’s smile vanished.

Caldrin looked delighted.

“Worth testing?”

Zee stepped forward.

“No.”

Everyone looked at him.

He stared at Khell.

“We are not guessing with people upstairs.”

Aurelia’s expression softened slightly.

Good.

Zee had learned.

Khell looked toward him.

“Then you lose.”

Zee shook his head.

“No.”

He looked at Ner.

“You said contract.”

“Yes.”

“Contracts have rules.”

Ner’s eyes sharpened.

“Yes.”

“Can you see them?”

“Some.”

“Find the part that says what Khell has to do.”

Ner approached the pillar.

Pure Golden Mana spread slowly over the runes.

Lines appeared.

Layers.

Conditions.

Ownership.

Payment.

Transfer.

Release.

Ner’s face twisted with disgust.

“He built people into trade terms.”

Aurelia’s jaw tightened.

“Can you break it?”

“Maybe.”

Zee immediately said, “Do not guess.”

Ner looked at him.

Then smiled faintly.

“Learning.”

“I hate you.”

“No, you do not.”

Ner kept reading.

Rez watched the corridor.

Caldrin watched Khell.

Aurelia held the rescued boy.

Zee listened to every heartbeat.

Then Ner stopped.

“There.”

A single line glowed Gold.

“What?” Rez asked.

Ner smiled.

“Khell does not have to voluntarily release them.”

Khell’s face changed.

Ner looked toward him.

“The contract says release occurs if ownership transfers.”

Silence.

Caldrin began laughing.

Khell struggled.

“No.”

Ner continued.

“If Khell no longer owns the cages…”

Aurelia understood.

“…the restraints open.”

Zee looked at Khell.

“Then sell them.”

Khell stared.

“To whom?”

Zee smiled.

“Me.”

Aurelia’s head snapped toward him.

“No.”

“Mother.”

“We are not purchasing our family.”

“I know.”

He looked at Ner.

“Can a trade be one coin?”

Ner examined the contract.

“Yes.”

Khell laughed bitterly.

“You think I will agree?”

Rez stepped forward.

“You already said everything has a price.”

Khell’s face hardened.

Caldrin pulled a copper coin from his pocket.

“Inflation has been cruel.”

Zee took it.

He held the coin toward Khell.

“One copper.”

“No.”

Zee looked toward Ner.

“Does he need to say yes?”

Ner studied the runes.

“No.”

Khell froze.

Ner smiled.

“He authorized standing transfers through registered brokers.”

Everyone slowly looked at Mara’s absence.

Then remembered.

Mara.

Former registered broker.

Still on the upper deck.

Rez grinned.

“Oh, that is beautiful.”

Aurelia almost smiled.

Zee ran.

⸻

Mara stared at the copper coin.

“You want me to what?”

“Transfer the cages.”

“To Zee?”

“Yes.”

“From Khell?”

“Yes.”

“Without Khell signing?”

Ner pointed toward the contract.

“You are still registered.”

Mara stared at the Golden copy of the relevant condition.

Then began laughing.

Not politely.

Not quietly.

Years of fear broke inside it.

“Khell wrote that clause.”

“Why?” Rez asked.

“So brokers could move stock when he was not present.”

Aurelia’s face hardened at the word.

Mara corrected herself.

“People.”

Then looked toward Zee.

“One copper?”

Zee held it out.

Mara took the coin.

“No.”

Zee frowned.

“What?”

She turned toward the nearest river window.

Then threw it into the water.

Zee stared.

Mara looked at him.

“No one buys them.”

Ner smiled.

“Then transfer ownership to no one.”

Mara placed her hand against the contract seal.

The red runes ignited.

Khell screamed below deck.

Every cage upstairs opened.

Iron doors released simultaneously.

Restraints fell.

Seraphine collapsed into Aurelia’s arms.

The other Veyrath survivors emerged.

No owners.

No buyers.

No cages.

Free.

Mara looked at the dead contract light fading beneath her hand.

Her voice broke.

“That is better.”

Zee nodded.

“Yes.”

Much better.

⸻

Khell was dragged onto the deck in chains.

The irony was not subtle.

Rez enjoyed it.

Ner enjoyed it more.

Caldrin enjoyed it most.

Zareth landed beside the ship, wings beating hard enough to keep himself above the river rather than placing his full weight on the damaged deck.

Seraphine saw him.

Then looked at Zee.

Then Aurelia.

“You have a Drakonis prince following your child.”

Zee frowned.

“He does not follow me.”

Zareth sent:

Lie.

Zee looked toward him.

“Not helping.”

Seraphine smiled weakly.

For the first time in years, perhaps.

Theron arrived aboard a smaller skiff with Vale.

The moment he saw Seraphine, everything stopped.

Seraphine stared.

Theron stared back.

“No.”

He climbed onto the deck before Vale could stop him.

Nearly fell.

Recovered.

“Seraphine.”

Her face crumpled.

“Theron?”

He reached her.

She hit him.

Hard.

Theron stumbled.

Zee stared.

Rez nodded.

“Sibling reunion.”

Seraphine hit him again.

“You left me!”

“I thought you died!”

“I was eight!”

“I know!”

“You were supposed to find me!”

“I tried!”

Then she threw both arms around him.

Theron held her.

Aurelia joined them.

Three siblings.

All believed dead by at least one of the others.

All alive.

No magic brought them back.

No power rewrote their losses.

They had survived separately.

That made the reunion hurt differently.

It was not undoing history.

It was discovering history had continued somewhere else.

Zee stood beside Rez and Ner.

Zareth hovered nearby.

Ner wiped one eye.

Rez noticed.

“Crying?”

“No.”

“You are.”

“River wind.”

“We are barely moving.”

Ner looked at him.

“I will throw you overboard.”

Zee smiled.

Then Aurelia looked toward him.

She extended one hand.

Zee walked over.

She pulled him into the embrace.

Theron laughed.

Seraphine looked down at Zee’s horns.

Then his face.

“So this is your son.”

Aurelia nodded.

Seraphine touched one Crimson scale near Zee’s temple.

He allowed it.

“He feels like Veyrath.”

“Partly.”

“And something else.”

“Several something elses.”

Zee frowned.

Everyone laughed.

He looked offended.

That made them laugh harder.

For one brief moment, the Red Passage stopped being a place where people had been priced.

It became the place where a family found one another again.

Then Zee looked toward Khell.

The broker sat bound against the mast.

Alive.

Furious.

Defeated.

Not the end of the world.

Not the mastermind behind every hunt.

Just one man who had made a fortune from suffering.

Aurelia followed Zee’s gaze.

“What do we do with him?” Zee asked.

Aurelia looked toward the freed captives.

“Not my decision alone.”

Theron nodded.

Seraphine’s expression became cold.

“Good.”

Khell looked toward Zee.

“You think this changes anything?”

Zee met his eyes.

“It changed them.”

He pointed toward the freed Veyrath.

“That is enough.”

Khell said nothing.

Zee turned away.

The answer surprised Aurelia.

Years ago, Zee would have needed the final word.

Needed violence to feel complete.

Now he understood something else.

Sometimes defeating an enemy mattered less than what you carried away from them.

Tonight, they carried people.

Names.

Family.

Proof that House Veyrath was not as dead as Aurelia had believed.

And a new problem.

Because seven rescued Veyrath survivors could no longer disappear quietly.

By sunrise, buyers would talk.

The River Market would talk.

The Ashen Talons would hear.

Drakonis might hear.

Victor might hear.

The world would learn that Aurelia Veyrath had stepped out of hiding.

Zee stood beside his mother as the three Red Passage ships drifted toward Vesper-controlled waters.

He looked at Theron.

Seraphine.

The other survivors.

Then Rez.

Ner.

Zareth.

His family had become larger in a single week.

That should have made him feel safer.

Instead, Zee understood exactly what it meant.

More people to love.

More people the world could threaten.

But also—

more people who could stand beside him when it tried.

And this time, the world had not hunted Zee Darkheart.

Zee had hunted it back.

He had found what it took.

And he had brought them home.

Crimson Dynasty — Chapters Forty & Forty-One

Chapter Forty

The Last Year of Childhood

House Vesper did not celebrate the return of House Veyrath.

Not publicly.

Lucien refused.

Too many people had escaped the Red Passage.

Too many buyers had seen Aurelia.

Too many brokers had heard the Veyrath name spoken aloud.

Trying to pretend nothing had happened would not protect anyone.

But announcing seven rescued survivors to every crown in the known world would be worse.

So Vesper did what it had always done best.

It made room.

Quietly.

Seven members of Veyrath blood had entered the rescue.

Seven returned free.

No cages.

No contracts.

No owners.

Theron walked through Vesper’s gates beside Aurelia instead of behind chains.

Seraphine came two steps later, glaring at every tower as though personally deciding whether she approved of the kingdom.

The others followed beneath Vesper protection.

Some wanted names restored.

Others did not.

One refused to sleep indoors.

Another refused to allow anyone to close a door behind him.

One of the rescued women spent the first night sitting in a garden until sunrise because grass beneath bare feet was apparently something captivity had made precious.

No one tried to hurry them.

Freedom did not make people whole by sunset.

It only gave them the right to decide what came next.

Zee understood that better than he expected.

He stood on the eastern balcony three days after their return and watched Theron argue with a healer below.

“You are supposed to use the cane,” the healer said.

“I am using it.”

“You are carrying it.”

“That counts.”

“It absolutely does not.”

Theron looked toward Aurelia for support.

She was sitting beneath an old cedar with Seraphine.

She did not help.

“Use the cane.”

Theron looked betrayed.

“You used to like me.”

“I used to be eight.”

“Children have better judgment.”

Seraphine snorted.

“You spent half our childhood throwing him out of trees.”

Theron pointed toward her.

“She remembers.”

Aurelia smiled.

Zee watched that smile longer than he meant to.

Ner leaned against the balcony beside him.

“You are doing it again.”

Zee looked over.

“What?”

“Watching your mother to make sure she is still happy.”

Zee frowned.

“I am not.”

Ner raised one eyebrow.

At ten, Nereus Azrion had somehow mastered the exact look Aurelia used when Zee lied badly.

Zee hated that.

“She lost them.”

Ner looked toward the garden.

“She found them.”

“That is not the same.”

“No.”

Zee looked at him.

Ner understood.

Finding people who had survived did not erase the years they were gone.

It did not erase cages.

Did not erase House Veyrath breaking apart.

Did not erase Victor.

Did not erase anything.

It simply meant grief had been wrong about one thing.

Some of them had lived.

Zee rested his arms against the stone railing.

“I thought getting them back would fix her.”

Ner was quiet.

Then:

“That was stupid.”

Zee turned sharply.

Ner continued before he could argue.

“Not because you wanted it.”

“Because people are not broken doors.”

Zee frowned.

“You have been spending too much time with my mother.”

“She likes me.”

“She likes everyone more than me.”

“That is because you are difficult.”

Zee shoved him.

Ner shoved back.

Neither moved much.

Behind them came Rez’s voice.

“If you both fall off that balcony, I am not explaining it.”

Zee looked back.

Rez crossed the room carrying two wooden practice blades.

His current body had stopped looking strange to Zee weeks ago.

That surprised him more than the magic itself had.

Rez was simply Rez.

Ten.

Fast.

Annoying.

His sword balance had settled.

His stride belonged to him again.

He tossed one blade toward Zee.

Zee caught it.

“No.”

“Yes.”

“I am busy.”

“You are staring at your mother from a balcony.”

“That is busy.”

Rez pointed the second blade toward the courtyard.

“Training.”

Ner smiled.

“I will watch.”

Rez looked at him.

“You are training too.”

Ner’s smile vanished.

“That seems unnecessary.”

“You told me yesterday my left side was slow.”

“It is.”

“Prove yours is better.”

Ner sighed.

“This friendship is exhausting.”

Zee looked toward the courtyard below.

Aurelia noticed the three boys.

“Do not break anything.”

Rez shouted down, “That depends on Zee.”

Zee looked offended.

“You changed age because of him,” Seraphine called from beside Aurelia. “I have known the boy three days and already believe that.”

Zee stared.

Aurelia had told her.

Of course she had.

He looked toward Rez.

“You told her?”

Rez shrugged.

“She is family.”

Seraphine cupped her hands around her mouth.

“And it is funny.”

Zee’s crimson eyes narrowed.

Seraphine smiled sweetly.

Zee liked her.

That was becoming a problem.

⸻

Damaris Khell did not receive a dramatic execution.

That disappointed Caldrin.

He admitted it freely.

The decision belonged to the seven people his trade had harmed directly.

Aurelia refused to take that choice from them.

Khell was tried under Vesper law for trafficking, illegal imprisonment, blood trade, magical coercion, and the purchase and sale of living supernatural captives within routes protected by Vesper treaties.

Morcant was charged separately.

Neither became a mystery.

Neither revealed a hidden throne behind the world.

Khell had buyers.

Morcant had money.

They had both profited from people other kingdoms preferred not to notice.

That was enough evil without making it grander.

Khell was sentenced to permanent confinement beneath Vesper wards after the surviving captives rejected immediate execution.

Not mercy.

Control.

They wanted names.

Ledgers.

Routes.

Buyers.

People still missing.

Khell knew too much to die quickly.

Morcant’s estate was stripped of every captive holding, collection ledger, restraint design, and blood contract Vesper could identify.

Several kingdoms protested the seizure.

Lucien sent each protest back with copies of purchase records.

Most became suddenly quiet.

Mara was not pardoned.

That mattered too.

She had helped destroy Khell’s ownership structure.

She had helped free people.

She had also once carried people into cages.

Both truths remained.

Lucien offered her no clean absolution.

Instead, she agreed to testify, identify routes, locate former buyers, and help Vesper dismantle trafficking paths she had once used.

Theron hated her.

Seraphine hated her differently.

One of the other rescued Veyrath survivors refused to be in the same room with her.

Mara accepted all three responses.

Zee asked her once whether she expected forgiveness.

She shook her head.

“No.”

“Then why stay?”

“Because leaving would be easier.”

Zee thought about that.

Then nodded.

Sometimes doing better did not mean being rewarded for it.

Sometimes it simply meant refusing to keep being worse.

⸻

The first rumor reached Vesper nine days later.

House Veyrath lives.

The second changed it.

Aurelia Darkheart is Veyrath.

The third became nonsense.

Aurelia had raised a hidden army of Dragon-Witches beneath Vesper.

The fourth claimed Zee had hatched from a Dragon egg.

The fifth insisted Zareth was actually Zee’s brother.

Zee nearly choked when Rez read that one aloud.

“No.”

Ner took the parchment.

“It gets worse.”

“How?”

“It says Queen Aurelia laid both eggs.”

Zee stared.

Zareth’s amusement exploded through the Dragon Link.

“No.”

Rez was already laughing.

Seraphine took the rumor sheet.

She read it once.

Then looked toward Aurelia.

“Mother would have enjoyed this.”

Aurelia covered her face.

Theron laughed until the healer threatened him.

Rumors were spreading.

That part could not be stopped.

House Veyrath was no longer safely dead in the imagination of the world.

Some survivors wanted that.

Theron did.

He refused to live under another name.

Seraphine did not.

Not yet.

She wanted the right to exist before she wanted recognition.

The other survivors divided between them.

Aurelia did not force unity.

House Veyrath had survived destruction.

It did not need to survive by becoming another cage.

Some stayed in Vesper.

Some planned eventually to leave.

Some wanted to search for more blood.

Others wanted nothing to do with Veyrath politics ever again.

Family grew.

It did not become simple.

Zee liked that.

He was learning simple answers were usually suspicious.

⸻

Near the end of autumn, Aurelia found Zee alone in the old training yard.

He was sitting on the ground with one knee raised, staring at his hands.

No Mana.

No scales spreading.

No horns burning.

Just hands.

Aurelia sat beside him.

“You are quiet.”

“I can do that.”

“Rarely.”

Zee looked toward her.

She smiled.

He looked away again.

“What are you thinking?”

“Rez.”

Aurelia waited.

Zee continued.

“I keep thinking about what I did.”

She did not tell him to stop.

That helped.

“I do not want to do it again.”

“Then do not.”

He frowned.

“You say that like I know how.”

“No.”

Aurelia looked toward the empty yard.

“I say it because not understanding a power does not mean you should poke it until someone suffers.”

Zee’s mouth twitched.

“That sounded like Caldrin.”

“Then I am deeply ashamed.”

He laughed.

Then became serious.

“What if it happens anyway?”

“Then you take responsibility for what happens.”

He looked at her.

“Not blame for everything.”

“Responsibility for what is yours.”

She touched one of his hands.

“Those are different.”

Zee thought of Khell.

Mara.

Morcant.

The hunters.

All the people whose choices had once felt like something Zee should somehow have prevented simply by existing differently.

He had spent years believing danger around him belonged to him.

Now he knew better.

Not completely.

Enough to start.

Aurelia continued.

“Fear is not control.”

“I know.”

“No.”

She looked at him.

“You are beginning to.”

Zee frowned.

“Same thing.”

“It is not.”

He sighed dramatically.

Aurelia smiled.

“You tried suppressing every Dragon reaction after Rez changed.”

Zee looked down.

“They got worse.”

“Yes.”

“Then Rez told me to listen.”

“And?”

“I hate when Rez is right.”

Aurelia laughed.

Zee smiled.

Then touched one developing horn.

“Sometimes the heat means anger.”

“Sometimes danger.”

“Sometimes Zareth.”

“Sometimes you.”

“Sometimes nothing I understand yet.”

Aurelia nodded.

“And now?”

“Now I notice before I explode.”

“Usually.”

Zee glared.

“Mother.”

“Usually is progress.”

He leaned against her.

At ten, he still did that.

Still fit beneath her arm.

Still allowed himself to.

Aurelia wrapped him close.

Zee listened to her heartbeat.

Not because his vampire nature forced him.

Because it was hers.

Steady.

Alive.

One day the world would take things from him no power could restore.

He did not know that future yet.

But he had already learned enough not to mistake survival for control over everything.

Some losses could not be undone.

Some people could only be protected while they were here.

That knowledge did not make him calm.

It made him present.

“Mother?”

“Yes?”

“I am glad they lived.”

Aurelia closed her eyes.

“So am I.”

“It does not fix what happened.”

“No.”

“But it matters.”

“Yes.”

Zee nodded.

That was enough.

⸻

Winter came early.

By the first snowfall, Rez had stopped training as though his body were something borrowed.

It was simply his.

He ran the western wall beside Zee and Ner every morning.

He complained when they cheated.

They cheated anyway.

Ner’s Golden Mana had begun developing precision rather than merely power. He could form thin barriers instead of massive spheres, thread Mana through damaged wards, and strike hard enough to remind everyone that rare did not mean delicate.

Zareth spent increasing stretches in Drakonis.

That had been difficult at first.

For Zee.

Not Zareth.

The Dragon Crown Prince had obligations.

Family.

Training.

Politics.

A kingdom that expected to know the young prince who had spent much of his earliest life beside a Darkheart child.

Zee hated the first three-week absence.

He slept badly.

Checked the Dragon Link constantly.

Pretended he did not.

When Zareth returned, he brought no apology.

Only a stolen Drakonis ceremonial banner tied around one horn.

Zee laughed for ten minutes.

The Link had changed too.

Distance no longer made it disappear entirely.

It stretched.

Thinned.

Carried impressions rather than clarity.

But it endured.

Zareth had his own life.

Zee was learning that love did not require keeping someone within reach every second.

He disliked the lesson.

Still learned it.

⸻

On the final night of the year, the core four climbed onto the western roof.

It had become tradition without anyone deciding it was.

Zee sat near the edge.

Ner beside him.

Rez sprawled behind them.

Zareth occupied half the roof.

Snow gathered along his crimson scales and melted whenever Teal-and-Gold Mana warmed beneath them.

Below, Vesper celebrated quietly.

Not Zee.

Not Veyrath.

Not victory.

The year ending.

That felt better.

Rez threw a small piece of ice at Ner.

Ner caught it with Golden Mana.

Then sent it back twice as fast.

It struck Rez in the forehead.

Rez sat up.

“That was magic.”

“Yes.”

“We said no magic.”

“You said no magic.”

“That is what we means.”

Ner looked toward Zee.

“Does it?”

“No.”

Rez stared at both.

“You are terrible people.”

Zareth sent through the Link:

Agree.

Zee looked at him.

“You are included.”

The Dragon Prince immediately withdrew agreement.

Zee laughed.

Then silence settled naturally.

No danger.

No alarm.

No approaching hunter.

Just the four of them.

Zee looked over Vesper.

He remembered being carried into these walls as an infant while kingdoms wanted his blood.

Remembered cages.

Assassins.

Frost.

Dreams.

The Vault.

His mother becoming a Dragon beneath the moon.

Sevran stepping from her past.

Rez changing because Zee lost control.

The Red Passage.

Theron.

Seraphine.

He had spent childhood running because adults believed the safest direction was always away.

Sometimes they were right.

Sometimes running had kept him alive.

But Zee was not the child Aurelia once carried between kingdoms anymore.

He had chased Khell.

Walked into Morcant’s estate.

Helped free people he had never met.

Learned to stop before rage chose for him.

Learned that fear could warn instead of rule.

Learned that family made him vulnerable.

Learned that family also made him harder to isolate.

Ner nudged his shoulder.

“What?”

Zee realized he had been staring.

“Nothing.”

“You look weird.”

“That is rude.”

“You are smiling at the city.”

Rez sat up.

“He does that when he is thinking too much.”

Zee turned.

“You both know too much about me.”

Rez smiled.

“That seems like your problem.”

Zareth lowered his head beside them.

The Link carried warmth.

Home.

Not a word.

Not exactly.

But close.

Zee looked at the three of them.

Then toward the rooms below where Aurelia sat with Theron and Seraphine.

House Veyrath breathed again.

Vesper still stood.

Drakonis still claimed its prince.

Victor still lived in Castle Darkheart.

The world still hunted what it feared.

Nothing was solved forever.

Zee was glad.

He did not want his life finished at ten.

Snow fell harder.

He reached one hand toward it.

A flake landed against a Crimson scale and vanished.

For the first ten years of Zee Darkheart’s life, the world had tried to decide what kind of creature he would become before he was old enough to decide anything for himself.

Witch.

Vampire.

Wolf.

Sleeping Dragon.

Prince.

Monster.

Abomination.

Weapon.

Miracle.

Threat.

Zee looked at his hand.

Then closed it.

The world could keep its names.

Childhood ended without a crown.

Without a prophecy.

Without another enemy appearing at the gate.

It ended with a boy sitting beside the people who had survived it with him.

A boy who knew more about himself than before.

A boy whose enemies knew more about him too.

A boy no longer small enough to carry away every time danger came.

Zee Darkheart had survived childhood.

And childhood had not survived him unchanged.

⸻

Chapter Forty-One

The Boys Who Hunt Back

Five Years Later

The vampire hit the ground hard enough to split the frozen earth.

Zee landed on him a heartbeat later.

At fifteen, there was nothing small about the impact.

The vampire rolled.

Zee’s claws caught only snow.

“Left!”

Ner’s voice.

Zee moved without looking.

A silver chain cut through the space where his throat had been.

Rez came through the trees on the opposite side.

His sword flashed once.

Blue-White Mana followed.

The chain froze from end to end.

The vampire holding it swore.

Rez smiled.

“Should have let go.”

He twisted his wrist.

The frozen chain snapped.

Ner raised both hands.

Pure Golden Mana spread between the surrounding trees—not a wall, but six thin planes of light angled precisely enough to close every escape without trapping the four boys inside.

The vampire looked around.

Then upward.

Mistake.

Zareth dropped through the winter canopy.

Fifteen years had turned the Crown Prince of Drakonis into something the forest itself seemed too small to contain.

His crimson Dragon body struck the clearing with controlled force.

Snow exploded outward.

Teal-and-Gold Mana burned beneath his scales.

Wings wider than entire cottages folded along his sides.

The fleeing vampire stopped.

Zee stood.

He was taller now.

Lean rather than bulky, but built by years of running, fighting, climbing, hunting, and surviving things designed to break him.

His horns had grown with him.

Crimson-black curves swept farther back from his temples now, still not fully mature but impossible to hide beneath ordinary hair.

Permanent Crimson scales patterned more of his body than they had at ten—along the sides of his neck, shoulders, ribs, forearms, and portions of his temples.

His fangs had matured.

So had the way people reacted when they saw them.

The vampire stared.

Recognition arrived.

Not from Zee’s face.

From everything else.

“No.”

Zee smiled.

“Yes.”

The vampire backed away.

“You are Darkheart.”

Rez walked into the clearing.

At fifteen, his current body moved with absolute familiarity.

No hesitation.

No recalculation.

His sword belonged in his hand.

His balance belonged beneath his feet.

Blue-White Mana moved around him with the kind of precision that came from years of choosing how to use what he already knew differently.

The vampire looked at him.

“Vesper.”

Rez inclined his head.

Ner stepped from between the trees.

His Golden Mana dimmed around the barriers but did not disappear.

He had grown taller too, his features sharper, confidence sitting on him more naturally now.

He wore no gloves.

Had not for years unless secrecy required them.

The vampire saw the Gold.

His expression worsened.

“Azrion.”

Ner smiled.

“You know all our names.”

“Everyone knows your names.”

Zee’s smile faded slightly.

That still felt strange sometimes.

Five years ago strangers had called him the Darkheart child.

Now hunters whispered Zee Darkheart before he entered rooms.

Some stories claimed he could tear souls out through blood.

False.

Some said he had killed armies.

Very false.

One tavern story insisted he turned into a black Dragon during every full moon.

Zareth had enjoyed that one.

Zee had not.

The vampire looked upward toward Zareth.

“And Drakonis.”

The Dragon Prince lowered his head.

The evolving Link pressed a clear intention against Zee.

He smells afraid.

Zee answered with an image.

Good.

Not because fear pleased him.

Because this vampire had spent six months trafficking missing village children between two border territories.

Fear seemed appropriate.

Zee looked toward the unconscious man beneath his boot.

“Where are the others?”

The vampire spat blood.

“Go to hell.”

Ner sighed.

“We rode three days for this.”

Rez looked toward Zee.

“Want me?”

“No.”

Zee crouched.

The vampire flinched.

Interesting.

Zee did nothing magical.

He simply looked at him.

“Six children disappeared from Deren Village.”

No response.

“Two from wolf territory.”

Still nothing.

“One human boy was found in a ditch.”

The vampire’s heartbeat changed.

Zee heard it.

His Wolf instinct noticed the scent shift.

His Vampire nature counted the pulse.

His Witch Mana stayed quiet.

Dragon heat moved gently beneath his scales.

Controlled.

No overlap.

No surge.

No accident.

Zee tilted his head.

“You did not kill him.”

The vampire stared.

Rez looked toward Zee.

“You sure?”

“No.”

The vampire smiled faintly.

Zee continued.

“But that bothered you.”

The smile vanished.

“There.”

Ner folded his arms.

“You always get creepy when you do that.”

Zee glanced toward him.

“You like it.”

“I did not say that.”

“You looked impressed.”

“I was looking at the prisoner.”

Rez sighed.

“Can both of you flirt after we find the children?”

Silence.

Zee turned.

Ner turned.

Rez blinked.

“What?”

Zee’s face warmed.

That was irritating.

Crimson Dragon energy responded faintly beneath the scales along his neck.

Ner saw.

His mouth almost smiled.

Almost.

“We were not flirting,” Zee said.

Rez looked between them.

“Fine.”

Ner added, “We were not.”

Zareth sent a burst of unmistakable amusement through the Link.

Zee looked upward.

“Do not start.”

The vampire stared.

“You people are insane.”

Rez looked down at him.

“That is rude.”

Then the other captured vampire groaned.

Back to work.

Zee crouched again.

“Where?”

The vampire refused to answer.

So Zee looked toward Ner.

Ner stepped closer.

Not to unlock anything.

His Golden Mana had become far more than a key over five years.

Thin lines of light spread from his fingers and passed over the vampire’s coat.

Not through flesh.

Across objects.

Metal.

Leather.

Dust.

Then stopped at one pocket.

Ner reached inside.

The vampire lunged.

Rez’s sword touched his throat immediately.

Ner removed a black wooden token.

A symbol carved into it.

Wolf territory.

Rez recognized it.

“Old mill.”

Ner nodded.

“North road.”

The vampire’s heartbeat betrayed him.

Zee smiled.

“Thank you.”

The man swore.

Zareth sent Zee an image.

Mill.

Trees.

Smoke.

Then another.

Movement to the west.

Zee looked up sharply.

“Zareth found it.”

Rez sheathed his sword.

“Alive?”

The Link carried distant heartbeats.

Small.

Many.

Zee’s expression hardened.

“Yes.”

Ner dropped the Golden barriers.

“Then why are we still talking?”

They moved.

⸻

The old mill sat beside a frozen river two miles north.

They did not approach from one side.

They had stopped doing that years ago.

Zareth stayed high enough to track exits.

Rez circled west.

Ner went east.

Zee took the river.

No one needed to explain the plan.

They had used versions of it dozens of times.

Zee moved across the ice without breaking stride.

At fifteen his vampire speed had become less wild.

More economical.

His Wolf nature handled balance.

Witch Mana softened impact.

Crimson Dragon durability absorbed whatever neither prevented.

He reached the mill wall.

Stopped.

Listened.

Nine captives.

Three guards.

One vampire.

Two humans.

One heartbeat inside the upper office.

Different.

Wolf.

Zee frowned.

Not every person inside belonged to the same enemy.

Good to remember.

He touched the stone.

A faint vibration moved through his horns.

The Dragon nature had become stronger over five years.

Far stronger.

Still unfinished.

The ridges beneath his shoulder blades had developed too.

Visible now beneath thin clothing when his Dragon energy surged.

Sometimes they hardened enough that Aurelia would stare at them and refuse to guess what came next.

No wings.

Not yet.

The Vault’s old judgment remained lodged inside Zee’s memory.

Four.

Three awake.

Whatever counted as complete had still not happened.

Zee did not force it.

He had learned better than that.

He sent an image through the Dragon Link.

Nine.

Zareth answered.

Roof clear.

Not words.

Not quite.

Intent sharpened by fifteen years of familiarity.

Rez appeared through a crack in the western wall.

Ner’s Gold glimmered once from the east.

Ready.

Zee entered.

The first guard never saw him.

Zee caught the man’s wrist before the knife cleared leather.

Turned.

Dropped him.

Second guard came from behind.

Rez intercepted.

Blue-White Mana struck the floor.

The man slipped.

Rez’s pommel hit his jaw.

Down.

The vampire lunged toward the rear door.

Ner closed it with a Golden plane.

The vampire rebounded.

Zee caught him.

“Children.”

The vampire snarled.

“You think saving nine matters?”

Zee’s face changed.

“Yes.”

The vampire hesitated.

That answer had not been dramatic enough.

Good.

Zee tightened his grip.

“Where is the wolf upstairs?”

The vampire smiled.

“Not ours.”

There.

Zee released him just enough for Rez to bind him.

Then took the stairs.

He found a girl in the upper office.

Sixteen, maybe.

Wolf.

Bruised.

Armed with half a broken chair leg.

She swung the moment Zee entered.

He caught it.

“Stop.”

She snarled.

“Get away from me.”

“I am not with them.”

“Everyone says that.”

Fair.

Zee released the chair leg and stepped back.

She saw the horns.

The scales.

Recognition hit.

Not reassurance.

Fear.

“You.”

Zee stopped.

He knew that tone.

Not Zee.

Not a person.

A story.

“The Tribrid.”

He hated that less than monster.

Still not enough.

“My name is Zee.”

The girl stared.

Below them something crashed.

Ner shouted, “Rez!”

Rez shouted back, “That was not me!”

Zareth roared outside.

The girl flinched.

Zee sighed.

“That is Zareth.”

Her eyes widened.

“Drakonis?”

“Yes.”

Another crash.

Zee added, “Probably him.”

The Dragon Link sent offense.

The girl slowly lowered the wood.

“You came for them?”

“For everyone.”

Her expression changed.

“I am not one of the kidnapped children.”

“I know.”

“You know?”

“You smell like wolf territory.”

She stared.

“That is invasive.”

Zee blinked.

Then laughed.

“Sorry.”

That disarmed her more than any display of power could have.

“My brother is downstairs,” she said.

Zee’s smile disappeared.

“Which one?”

“Little.”

“Brown hair.”

“Scar over his chin.”

Zee listened.

Found the heartbeat.

“Alive.”

The girl almost collapsed.

She caught herself against the desk.

Zee did not touch her without permission.

Another lesson.

“They took him four days ago.”

“I followed.”

“They caught me yesterday.”

Zee nodded.

“So this was your hunt too.”

She looked at him.

He understood.

The world did not revolve around him.

Five years had made that truth instinctive.

He gestured toward the stairs.

“Come on.”

⸻

By midday the missing children were outside.

All alive.

Cold.

Hungry.

Terrified.

Alive.

Ner used Golden Mana only when one restraint refused to open normally.

Rez organized blankets and water before anyone asked.

Zareth remained at a distance because several children were more frightened of a massive Dragon than reassured by him.

He did not take offense.

Much.

The Dragon Link carried mild insult anyway.

Zee sat beside the smallest boy from wolf territory.

The boy kept staring at his horns.

Finally:

“Do they hurt?”

“Sometimes.”

“Can I touch one?”

Zee looked toward Aurelia in his memory.

Then toward the boy.

“Why?”

The child shrugged.

“I want to know if it feels like bone.”

Zee smiled.

“Okay.”

The boy touched the horn carefully.

“Hard.”

“Yes.”

“Cool.”

“Sometimes.”

The girl upstairs—his sister—stood nearby watching.

Her suspicion had not vanished.

Good.

Trust should not always be instant.

A rider appeared on the southern road.

Then another.

Local guards.

Not Vesper.

Not Zee’s.

They had their own authorities.

Their own responsibilities.

Zee stood.

The lead guard saw him.

Stopped.

The entire patrol slowed.

Recognition again.

One whispered something.

Zee heard.

“Darkheart.”

The leader dismounted carefully.

“We received reports of fighting.”

Rez approached.

“Trafficking ring.”

Ner pointed toward the captives.

“Three perpetrators alive.”

The guard looked between the boys.

Then toward Zareth.

Then back to Zee.

“You did this?”

The girl from wolf territory answered first.

“They did.”

Zee looked at her.

She shrugged.

“Truth.”

The guard’s expression became complicated.

Fear.

Relief.

Political calculation.

Zee knew that face too.

He had seen it on kings.

Hunters.

Village leaders.

People deciding whether Zee Darkheart was a threat before remembering he had just saved children.

The guard bowed.

Not deeply.

Not royal.

Respect without submission.

“Thank you.”

Zee blinked.

That still happened rarely enough to surprise him.

“You are welcome.”

The guard looked toward the prisoners.

“We take custody.”

Rez asked, “Under whose authority?”

“Border Council.”

Rez nodded.

“Send the records to House Vesper and the wolf council.”

The guard looked at him more closely.

“Prince Rez.”

“Yes.”

Recognition there too.

Different.

Political.

Established.

Rez no longer needed age explained to anyone who mattered.

He was simply fifteen-year-old Rez Vesper, Crown Prince, and apparently terrifying to bureaucrats.

The guard nodded quickly.

“Of course.”

Ner whispered to Zee.

“He likes that.”

Rez heard.

“I do.”

Zee smiled.

Then the rescued wolf girl approached.

“What happens to you now?”

Zee frowned.

“What do you mean?”

“You go home?”

“Eventually.”

“You are not from here.”

“No.”

“Then why were you hunting them?”

Zee looked toward the children.

Simple question.

Complicated answer.

Because someone had asked Vesper for help.

Because trafficking routes crossed old Veyrath maps.

Because Rez had pushed for intervention at council.

Because Ner found inconsistencies in missing-person wards.

Because Zareth spotted one transport wagon from the sky.

Because five years had taught them not every battle needed to begin at their own doorstep.

Zee shrugged.

“They were hurting people.”

The girl stared.

“That is it?”

“Is that not enough?”

She had no answer.

Zee turned away.

Across the frozen field, Ner was helping a child tie a blanket correctly.

His hair had fallen into his eyes.

He pushed it back impatiently.

Zee looked a little too long.

Ner glanced up.

Caught him.

Zee immediately looked elsewhere.

Too late.

A small smile appeared on Ner’s face.

Not mocking.

Worse.

Knowing.

Zee’s horns warmed.

Not danger.

He glared at them as though they had betrayed him personally.

Rez appeared beside him.

“You should really learn a different reaction.”

Zee did not look over.

“I do not know what you are talking about.”

“Of course.”

“Say anything and I will throw you in the river.”

Rez smiled.

“Fifteen years old and still terrible at lying.”

Zee elbowed him.

Rez elbowed back.

Zareth’s amusement crossed the Link.

Zee looked around at all three of them.

Fifteen.

Five more years of arguments.

Missions.

Training.

Scars.

Victories.

Mistakes.

No longer children adults carried from danger.

Not adults either.

Not finished.

Not close.

The world had changed around them.

Veyrath lived openly enough now that some kingdoms acknowledged its survivors.

Drakonis had learned to share its Crown Prince with a bond it still did not fully understand.

Vesper had become harder, richer, and more politically important after years of refusing to surrender the people under its protection.

Victor remained at Darkheart.

The black-robed observers remained somewhere beyond sight.

Hunters still came.

Bounties still appeared.

Stories about Zee multiplied faster than truth.

But something fundamental had reversed.

At ten, Zee had begun walking toward the world.

At fifteen, the world had started learning what that meant.

He looked toward the captured traffickers.

Then the rescued children.

Then Ner.

Rez.

Zareth.

Zee Darkheart smiled.

The boy they had once hunted was gone.

What stood in his place was still fifteen.

Still learning.

Still afraid of some things.

Still capable of making terrible decisions when angry.

Still his mother’s son.

Still Rez’s brother.

Still Ner’s best friend.

Still linked to Zareth by something neither kingdom could name.

But prey?

No.

That part of childhood was over.

And the world was beginning to understand the difference.
