Crimson Dynasty — Chapters Forty-Two & Forty-Three

Chapter Forty-Two

The Price of Interference

The first argument waiting for Zee Darkheart at House Vesper wore silk.

He preferred knives.

Knives were honest.

Knives did not sit beneath blue-white Vesper banners drinking expensive wine while explaining why eighteen kidnapped children should have remained somebody else’s problem.

Zee stood against the western wall of the royal council chamber with his arms crossed.

His travel coat was still damp from mountain snow.

A shallow cut beneath his jaw had already closed.

Permanent Crimson scales patterned the sides of his neck, disappeared beneath his collar, then returned along both forearms.

His crimson-black horns made pretending he was an ordinary visiting prince impossible.

That was fine.

He had stopped trying years ago.

Across the chamber, six Vesper nobles argued around the council table.

King Lucien sat at its head.

Rez sat to his right.

Not behind him.

Not watching.

Participating.

The fifteen-year-old Crown Prince had changed clothes after returning from the mountains, but nothing else about him suggested rest. His black hair remained wind-tossed, and Blue-White Mana occasionally flickered around two fingers whenever someone said something especially stupid.

It had happened four times.

Zee was counting.

Councilor Darius Hane struck one palm against the table.

“The Northern Border Council has formally protested.”

Lucien said nothing.

Hane continued.

“Prince Rez entered disputed territory with armed companions, apprehended licensed contractors, seized private documents, and transferred detainees without authorization.”

Zee lifted one eyebrow.

“Licensed kidnappers.”

Every head turned.

Rez closed his eyes.

Lucien looked toward Zee.

“I was handling that.”

“I improved it.”

Ner, seated near the opposite end of the table with three recovered ledgers spread before him, whispered, “You did not.”

Zee looked at him.

Ner did not look back.

That somehow made Zee look longer.

Five years had sharpened Nereus Azrion in ways Zee was still becoming embarrassingly aware of.

His face had lost the roundness of childhood.

His Pure Golden Mana moved with restrained confidence instead of flashing at every irritation.

His hands—

Zee stopped.

Why was he looking at Ner’s hands?

Rez cleared his throat.

Zee immediately faced the table.

Rez’s mouth twitched.

Traitor.

Councilor Hane continued.

“The point remains. Whatever those mercenaries did, Vesper cannot simply cross every border because Zee Darkheart decides someone deserves saving.”

Zee’s jaw tightened.

Lucien spoke before he could.

“They did not enter under Zee’s authority.”

Hane looked toward Rez.

Lucien continued.

“They entered under mine.”

That silenced several nobles.

Rez looked at his father.

Lucien did not look back.

He was letting the statement stand.

The mission had begun with requests transmitted through Vesper.

That made the consequences Vesper’s too.

Another noble, Lady Merrow, leaned forward.

“And now the Northern Council threatens to suspend grain passage through the upper roads.”

Theron Veyrath sat near the rear windows and muttered, “Then perhaps their grain should learn to walk around us.”

Seraphine, beside him, smiled.

“You would be terrible at diplomacy.”

“I survived captivity.”

“That did not make you charming.”

“It should have.”

Aurelia sat between them.

Five years of having both siblings back had not made their arguments less exhausting.

It had made her better at ignoring them.

She looked toward Zee instead.

He recognized the warning.

Do not explode at politicians.

He had received that look many times.

Zee sighed.

Rez leaned over the table.

“What exactly does the Northern Border Council want?”

Hane passed him a sealed letter.

Rez broke the wax.

Read.

His expression changed.

“Return of the prisoners.”

Ner looked up.

“Of course.”

Rez continued.

“Return of all seized property.”

“The ledgers?” Ner asked.

“Yes.”

“No.”

Several nobles looked toward him.

Ner calmly placed one hand over the nearest book.

Pure Golden Mana flickered across the cover.

“No.”

Councilor Hane frowned.

“Nereus, the documents were taken beyond Vesper jurisdiction.”

“They document the sale of children.”

“They allegedly document—”

“They document it.”

Ner looked directly at him.

“I spent six hours reading them.”

Hane’s expression hardened.

“That does not make you a court.”

Ner smiled faintly.

“No.”

Golden Mana formed along the edge of one page.

Hidden writing rose from the parchment.

Names.

Payments.

Village marks.

Transfer dates.

Then vanished when Ner lowered his hand.

“It makes me capable of reading what somebody tried to hide.”

Zee smiled.

Hane did not.

Good.

Rez folded the demand.

“What else?”

Lucien answered.

“The Northern Council is sending a representative.”

“When?”

“Tomorrow.”

Rez frowned.

“They sent the complaint before the children were even back in their villages.”

Silence.

That mattered.

The mountain officials had barely taken custody before the protest arrived.

Too fast.

Zee pushed away from the wall.

“So they already knew.”

Lady Merrow looked toward him.

“Knew what?”

“That the mercenaries would be arrested.”

Rez looked at Zee.

Then the letter.

Ner stopped moving.

Lucien’s eyes sharpened.

Councilor Hane said, “Or they reacted quickly.”

Ner opened the recovered ledger again.

“No.”

His Golden Mana passed over a payment line.

“There were scheduled reporting intervals.”

Rez stood.

He crossed to Ner.

Zee joined them without invitation.

No one bothered protesting.

The four had learned years ago that trying to keep Zee out of a mystery usually resulted in him standing closer.

Ner pointed to several marks near the bottom of the page.

“Every three days.”

Rez frowned.

“These are accounting notations.”

“No.”

Ner traced one symbol.

Gold gathered inside it.

The ink resisted.

His eyes narrowed.

“Mana.”

The royal witch, standing near Lucien, approached.

“Contract binding?”

“Maybe.”

Ner looked closer.

Then corrected himself.

“No.”

Zee smiled faintly.

Ner had learned that from Aurelia.

Never let maybe pretend to be certainty.

Ner continued.

“Something is layered beneath the ink.”

“Can you open it?” Lucien asked.

“Yes.”

Zee looked at him.

Ner’s expression changed.

“Probably.”

“Better.”

Ner rolled his eyes.

He placed both hands against the ledger.

Pure Golden Mana did not flood the room.

It narrowed.

Fine threads slipped between ordinary ink and the spellwork beneath it.

One layer.

Then another.

The council chamber quieted.

Zee watched Ner work.

Really work.

No door.

No ancient Vault.

No convenient Golden key waiting for him.

This was delicate magic.

His magic.

Ner’s fingers separated.

The hidden layer rose.

Blue-black symbols appeared beneath the payment records.

Rez recognized part of the structure first.

“Oath marks.”

The royal witch nodded.

“Silence binding.”

Aurelia stood.

“On whom?”

Ner followed the magical threads.

His face hardened.

“The mercenaries.”

Zee’s Crimson eyes narrowed.

“Meaning?”

“If they identify whoever hired them…”

Ner stopped.

Everyone waited.

He looked at Lucien.

“The spell punishes them.”

“How?”

“I need the prisoner.”

Rez immediately turned toward the guards.

“Bring Kest.”

⸻

Rovan Kest had led the mountain mercenaries.

At fifteen, Zee had met enough hired killers to know several types.

Men who loved money.

Men who loved violence.

Men who loved pretending money made the violence less personal.

Kest belonged somewhere between the first two.

He entered the council chamber with iron around both wrists and resentment in every step.

His eyes found Zee.

“You.”

Zee smiled.

“Me.”

Rez looked toward him.

Zee shrugged.

“He started it.”

Kest sat.

Ner placed the ledger on the table before him.

The mercenary’s face changed.

Tiny.

Enough.

Zee heard the heartbeat betray him.

Ner did too, in another way.

Golden Mana had already begun tracing the oath embedded around Kest’s magical signature.

Ner asked, “Who hired you?”

Kest opened his mouth.

Nothing came out.

His throat seized.

He grabbed his neck.

Zee moved instinctively.

Ner raised one hand.

“Don’t.”

Zee stopped.

Kest convulsed.

Thin black lines appeared beneath the skin of his throat.

Ner cursed.

Pure Golden Mana snapped around Kest’s neck without touching him.

A barrier inside a barrier.

The black lines stopped advancing.

The mercenary dragged in air.

Lucien stood.

“Can you remove it?”

Ner did not answer immediately.

His entire attention had gone inward.

Reading.

Adjusting.

Zee saw sweat form along his temple.

“Ner?”

“Quiet.”

Zee went quiet.

Immediately.

Rez noticed.

So did Seraphine.

Neither commented.

Ner’s Golden Mana divided into dozens of thin threads.

They slipped through the curse pattern.

One by one.

No brute force.

No dramatic explosion.

Precision.

The black lines withdrew.

Kest collapsed forward against the table.

Breathing.

Alive.

Ner lowered his hands.

The room remained silent.

Then Kest looked at him.

“What did you do?”

Ner sat back.

“Stopped it.”

“Can you break it?”

“Eventually.”

Kest laughed bitterly.

“You don’t know what you’re touching.”

Ner stared.

“Neither did the people who tried kidnapping my mother when I was three.”

Kest’s smile vanished.

Ner leaned forward.

“I learned.”

Zee felt something warm and fierce in his chest.

Pride.

Mostly.

Probably.

Rez saw his face.

Again.

Zee glared before he could say anything.

Rez looked innocent.

Lucien turned toward Kest.

“You knew you were bound.”

“Yes.”

“Who did it?”

Kest’s jaw locked.

The black lines twitched.

Ner immediately stabilized them.

Kest looked toward him.

Fear entered his eyes.

Not of Ner.

Of the spell.

Ner spoke calmly.

“Do not answer yet.”

Zee’s gaze moved toward Kest’s hands.

Scars along the fingers.

Old sword calluses.

One missing nail.

He had hurt people.

He had helped cage children.

Zee knew that.

Still, watching a spell squeeze the man’s throat because someone richer wanted silence made something inside Zee turn colder.

Predators could also be owned.

That did not make them innocent.

It made the world uglier.

Rez asked, “Can the oath be traced?”

Ner looked toward the ledger.

“Maybe to whoever anchored it.”

“Without making Kest speak?”

“Yes.”

That answer changed the room.

Lucien looked at his son.

Rez understood.

“We do not need a confession yet.”

Councilor Hane frowned.

“You are proposing magical intrusion into a contract cast outside Vesper.”

Rez looked toward him.

“I am proposing identifying who used magic to prevent testimony about kidnapped children.”

“That distinction will not impress the Northern Council.”

Rez’s expression became calm.

The kind of calm Zee knew meant someone was about to lose an argument.

“Then tomorrow they can explain why.”

Zee smiled.

Lucien’s mouth almost did.

⸻

Zareth left before sunset.

Not because of the council.

Because Drakonis summoned its Crown Prince.

A royal Dragon messenger arrived in true form above Vesper carrying a sealed strip of black-red metal from the Dragon Queen.

Zareth read it.

Then looked toward Zee.

The Dragon Link opened between them.

Mountain peaks.

A circular Drakonis council chamber.

Older Dragons.

Tension.

A border flame.

Responsibility.

Zee understood enough.

“How long?”

Zareth’s answer came as an image of three sunsets.

Then uncertainty.

Zee sighed.

“Go.”

Zareth lowered his head.

Five years ago Zee would have made the word sound wounded.

Now he meant it.

Mostly.

The Link carried warmth.

Then an image of Zee standing beside Ner in the council chamber.

Zee froze.

Zareth sent intense amusement.

Zee’s crimson eyes narrowed.

“I hate you.”

Ner, several yards away, looked over.

“What?”

“Not you.”

Rez walked past.

“He means Zareth.”

Ner nodded.

“That does not explain the face.”

Zee pointed at both of them.

“Neither of you speak.”

Rez smiled.

Zareth launched from the Vesper terrace.

Teal-and-Gold Mana rolled behind his wings.

He climbed through cloud and disappeared east.

The Link thinned.

Did not vanish.

Zee watched only until he was gone.

Then turned back toward the palace.

Progress.

Ner fell into step beside him.

“You are better at that.”

“Everyone keeps saying that.”

“Because you used to become unbearable.”

“I was never unbearable.”

Ner stared.

Zee looked ahead.

“Fine.”

They walked several steps.

Their shoulders brushed.

Neither moved away immediately.

Zee noticed.

Then noticed Ner noticing.

He moved half an inch.

Ner smiled.

Zee hated being fifteen.

⸻

Ner broke the first oath layer shortly before midnight.

Not completely.

Enough.

The magic did not point toward a hidden cult.

It did not reveal an ancient enemy.

It revealed a bureaucrat.

Councilor Maelor Renn.

Senior representative of the Northern Border Council.

The man already traveling toward Vesper to demand the mercenaries’ return.

Rez stared at the revealed magical signature.

Then at Lucien.

“He hired them.”

Lucien remained unreadable.

“Evidence suggests he anchored the contract.”

“That is diplomatic language for yes.”

“It is accurate language.”

Rez exhaled.

Ner sat nearby drinking water.

His hands trembled faintly from the work.

Zee saw.

He crossed the room and placed a cup closer.

Ner looked up.

“I have one.”

“This one is closer.”

Ner stared at him.

Zee immediately regretted speaking.

“Just drink it.”

Ner smiled.

“Yes, Your Highness.”

“Do not.”

Ner drank.

Rez watched both of them.

Zee refused to acknowledge him.

Aurelia entered from the adjoining chamber.

“Renn arrives tomorrow.”

Zee looked toward the glowing oath signature.

“Good.”

Something in his voice changed.

Aurelia heard it.

So did Rez.

Zee looked down at the name.

A man with political authority had helped abduct children.

Had paid mercenaries.

Had silenced those same mercenaries with magic.

And now he was walking voluntarily into Vesper expecting law to protect him.

Zee’s horns warmed.

Not uncontrolled.

Not yet.

He smiled faintly.

That was what bothered Aurelia.

“Zee.”

He looked at her.

“What?”

“You are enjoying this.”

The smile disappeared.

“No.”

Aurelia said nothing.

Zee looked away.

He had not hurt Renn.

Had not even met him.

But the thought of watching the man discover that his position would not save him—

That felt good.

Too good.

Rez folded the evidence.

“Tomorrow is not a hunt.”

Zee looked toward him.

“No?”

“No.”

Rez’s expression sharpened.

“Tomorrow he walks into my father’s court.”

A small smile appeared.

“Which is worse.”

Zee laughed.

Aurelia closed her eyes.

Ner looked between them.

“Both of you are concerning.”

Rez stood.

“Get some sleep.”

Zee stared.

“You are ordering me?”

“I am Crown Prince.”

“You say that when you have no argument.”

“I also say it when I am Crown Prince.”

Ner rose.

“He is right.”

Zee looked betrayed.

“You too?”

Ner passed close enough that his hand brushed Zee’s wrist.

Accidental.

Probably.

Zee’s Dragon scales warmed beneath the contact.

Ner noticed.

His eyes lifted.

For one strange heartbeat neither spoke.

Then Ner continued walking.

Rez waited until he left.

Zee immediately said, “Don’t.”

Rez grinned.

“I said nothing.”

“You have the face.”

“What face?”

Zee threw a cushion at him.

Rez caught it.

Aurelia laughed despite herself.

Tomorrow Councilor Maelor Renn would arrive expecting frightened children, compromised mercenaries, and a foreign king reluctant to risk trade over villagers no powerful House claimed.

Instead he would find Crown Prince Rez Vesper waiting with evidence.

Nereus Azrion waiting with the magic Renn thought could never be opened.

And Zee Darkheart waiting with something far more dangerous than rage.

Choice.

Because at fifteen Zee was beginning to understand that being able to hurt someone was easy.

Deciding what they deserved was harder.

And he was not yet certain which part he enjoyed more.

Chapter Forty-Three

What Monsters Deserve

Councilor Maelor Renn smiled when he entered Vesper’s royal chamber.

Zee disliked him immediately.

That meant nothing.

Zee had disliked people for terrible reasons before.

Their voices.

Their perfume.

The way they chewed.

One diplomat from the western witch territories had once made Zee furious simply because he breathed loudly through his nose.

Aurelia had informed him that this was not sufficient cause for violence.

Zee still considered the matter undecided.

So he ignored instinct.

Mostly.

Renn wore dark green robes embroidered with the silver lines of the Northern Border Council.

No weapons.

Four attendants.

Two legal scribes.

One witch adviser.

No visible fear.

He bowed to Lucien.

“Your Majesty.”

Then Rez.

“Crown Prince.”

Rez nodded.

Renn looked toward Aurelia.

“Queen Darkheart.”

Aurelia gave him nothing.

Then Zee.

The smile paused.

Only briefly.

“Prince Darkheart.”

Zee leaned against a pillar.

“Councilor.”

Renn’s eyes shifted toward Ner.

Ner sat beside the recovered ledger with one hand resting across its cover.

“Nereus Azrion.”

“Ner.”

The correction came automatically from Zee.

Silence.

Ner looked toward him.

Zee realized what he had done.

Renn’s eyebrow rose.

Rez looked down at his notes with suspicious concentration.

Ner smiled.

“Councilor.”

Zee decided he hated everyone.

Lucien spoke.

“You requested the immediate return of six prisoners currently held under Vesper protection.”

“Seven.”

Rez looked up.

“Six survived the mountain capture.”

Renn’s expression did not change.

“The seventh was not among the group you intercepted.”

Zee’s crimson eyes sharpened.

Rez went very still.

Ner looked toward the ledger.

Renn had just made a mistake.

A small one.

But good mistakes rarely announced themselves.

Rez asked, “How do you know?”

Renn paused.

“Know what?”

“How many were assigned.”

The councilor smiled.

“I read the preliminary report.”

“No.”

Rez folded his hands.

“The report listed six captured.”

Renn’s attendants shifted.

Lucien said nothing.

Rez continued.

“It never listed total company strength.”

Renn’s smile weakened.

“Then I was informed separately.”

“By whom?”

“A border official.”

“Name?”

Renn’s jaw tightened.

“This is becoming tedious.”

Zee smiled.

“Already?”

Aurelia looked toward him.

He stopped.

Rez continued.

“You demanded prisoners, seized documents, and reparations.”

“Yes.”

“You accused Vesper of unlawful intervention.”

“Correct.”

“Then answer the question.”

Renn looked at Lucien.

“Do you routinely permit your son to interrogate foreign representatives?”

Lucien’s expression remained calm.

“When they address matters within his authority.”

Rez’s gaze did not leave Renn.

Zee felt a little burst of pride.

Different from the feeling around Ner.

Much easier to understand.

Brother.

Renn adjusted his robes.

“The Northern Council received private reports.”

Rez nodded.

“Good.”

“Then produce them.”

Renn’s face hardened.

“No.”

“Then we continue.”

He gestured toward the side doors.

Rovan Kest entered.

Renn’s expression did not change.

His heartbeat did.

Zee heard it.

One hard acceleration.

Then controlled.

There.

Kest saw Renn.

Hatred entered his face.

The black oath lines beneath his throat immediately began darkening.

Ner moved.

Pure Golden Mana appeared around Kest before pain could take him.

Renn’s witch adviser stared.

“What is that?”

Ner looked toward her.

“Your Councilor’s work.”

Renn scoffed.

“Absurd.”

Ner raised one hand.

The recovered ledger opened itself.

Pages turned.

The hidden oath structure rose above them in Golden outline.

Not Golden because the original magic was his.

Golden because Ner was exposing its shape.

At the center sat Renn’s magical signature.

The councilor’s face finally changed.

Not fear.

Calculation.

“How theatrical.”

Ner smiled.

“Thank you.”

“It proves nothing.”

“It proves the oath was anchored through your Mana.”

“Forgery.”

Ner tilted his head.

“Would you like to touch it?”

Renn said nothing.

“Thought not.”

Rez stood.

“Rovan Kest, do you recognize Councilor Renn?”

Kest opened his mouth.

The oath tightened.

Ner’s Golden Mana tightened with it.

Not against Kest.

Around the curse.

Holding it apart.

Sweat ran down Ner’s face.

Zee straightened.

This was hurting him.

Not physically.

Straining.

Ner did not need rescue.

Zee knew that.

He still hated watching.

Kest forced one word.

“Yes.”

The oath erupted.

Ner grunted.

Golden Mana flashed violently.

Zee took one step forward.

Ner looked at him.

“No.”

Zee stopped.

The word irritated him.

He obeyed anyway.

Ner separated another layer.

The curse quieted.

Renn’s witch adviser had gone pale.

She looked toward her employer.

“You said those marks were employment restrictions.”

Renn did not look at her.

Rez heard.

“So you knew about them.”

The adviser swallowed.

“I knew there were oaths.”

“Did you know they punished testimony?”

“No.”

Renn snapped, “Enough.”

Rez turned toward him.

“No.”

The single word landed differently from Ner.

Royal.

Cold.

Renn’s face darkened.

“You are fifteen.”

No one moved.

Zee’s expression sharpened.

Rez did not.

“And?”

“You presume to lecture officials who held office before you were born.”

Rez smiled.

“Then you had plenty of time to learn not to kidnap children.”

Zee laughed.

Lucien’s mouth moved once.

Aurelia looked toward the ceiling.

Seraphine, seated among the observers, whispered to Theron:

“I like him.”

Theron nodded.

“Good boy.”

Rez continued.

“Kest. Who hired your company?”

Ner looked toward the mercenary.

“Slowly.”

Kest breathed.

The Golden containment tightened around the oath.

He looked at Renn.

Then said:

“Councilor…”

The curse struck.

Ner caught it.

“…Renn.”

The chamber erupted.

Vesper nobles began speaking at once.

Renn’s attendants moved.

Lucien raised one hand.

Silence returned.

Rez’s eyes remained on Renn.

“Why?”

Kest laughed bitterly.

“Land.”

Renn shouted, “This is coerced testimony.”

Kest turned toward him.

“You promised payment.”

The oath attacked again.

Ner hissed through his teeth.

Zee saw his fingers shake.

His horns warmed.

Aurelia watched him.

Choice.

Zee stayed where he was.

Kest continued.

“Three villages kept fighting over winter pasture.”

“Renn wanted the northern road.”

“He paid us to take children from all three.”

“Leave signs pointing at the others.”

“Keep them angry.”

“Keep the road unstable.”

Rez’s face became disgusted.

“For what?”

“Council purchase.”

Lady Merrow whispered, “He planned to buy the route after the villages collapsed.”

Kest nodded.

Renn moved suddenly.

Not toward Kest.

Toward Ner.

His hand struck the ledger.

Mana flashed.

The hidden oath structure ignited.

Ner shouted.

Golden light buckled.

Kest collapsed.

Black lines raced up his throat.

Renn’s witch adviser screamed.

“I did not—”

Ner dropped to one knee.

Zee moved.

This time nobody stopped him.

He crossed the chamber before Renn’s guards understood he had left the pillar.

One hand closed around the councilor’s wrist.

The other caught his throat.

Renn hit the council table.

Hard.

Crimson Mana shook the wood.

The man’s eyes widened.

Zee smelled fear.

There.

Warm.

Sharp.

Satisfying.

Renn tried casting.

Zee squeezed his wrist.

Bone cracked.

Renn screamed.

And Zee smiled.

He saw it reflected in Renn’s eyes.

That was the part that frightened him.

Not the broken bone.

Not the screaming.

The pleasure.

This man had ordered children taken.

Had turned villages against each other.

Had built magic into hired killers so they could not expose him.

Had just triggered that magic while Ner was holding it apart.

Zee wanted to hurt him again.

Not to stop him.

Not because it was necessary.

Because he deserved it.

At least Zee thought he did.

Renn’s pulse raced beneath Zee’s hand.

Wolf instinct smelled weakness.

Vampire hunger sharpened around blood.

Crimson Dragon heat moved through the scales on Zee’s forearm.

His Witch Mana waited.

Four natures.

Three awake.

Every part of him capable of making Renn’s next moment unbearable.

Zee leaned closer.

“You hurt him.”

Renn struggled.

“He exposed restricted—”

Zee squeezed.

Another scream.

“Wrong answer.”

Aurelia’s voice crossed the chamber.

“Zee.”

He heard her.

Did not stop.

Ner’s voice came next.

“Zee.”

That did.

He looked back.

Ner remained on one knee beside Kest.

Pale.

Angry.

Alive.

Golden Mana still holding the oath apart.

Their eyes met.

Ner shook his head once.

Not frightened of Zee.

Not begging for Renn.

Something simpler.

Not this.

Rez stood beside Ner.

His sword remained sheathed.

“Zee.”

Zee looked back at Renn.

He could crush his throat.

Nothing in the room could stop him before it happened.

That knowledge felt dangerous.

Renn saw it.

For one second the polished official vanished.

Just a terrified man underneath Zee’s hand.

Zee liked that too.

He released him.

Renn collapsed against the table coughing.

The chamber stayed silent.

Zee stared at his own hand.

Then turned away.

“Fix Kest.”

Ner understood.

He returned to the oath.

Zee walked back toward his pillar.

Nobody blocked him.

Nobody thanked him.

Good.

He did not want thanks.

His hand was still tingling from breaking Renn’s wrist.

That bothered him.

Not enough.

That bothered him more.

⸻

Ner dismantled the oath completely twelve minutes later.

Not every mercenary binding in existence.

Only Kest’s.

One person.

One spell.

Careful.

When the final black line disappeared from Kest’s throat, the mercenary inhaled.

Then laughed.

A broken, exhausted sound.

“Gone?”

Ner sat back against the table.

“Yes.”

Kest touched his neck.

“Gone.”

Rez crouched beside him.

“Can you testify freely?”

Kest looked toward Renn.

“Yes.”

Renn sat under Vesper guard with his broken wrist bound.

His diplomatic composure had not returned.

Neither had his authority.

Rez stood.

“Councilor Maelor Renn.”

Renn looked toward him.

“You entered Vesper under diplomatic sanctuary.”

Renn smiled faintly.

“At least one person here remembers law.”

Rez’s expression did not change.

“You then activated lethal coercive magic against a protected witness inside my father’s royal chamber.”

The smile disappeared.

Renn’s legal scribe whispered something urgently.

Too late.

Rez continued.

“Under Vesper covenant law, sanctuary does not protect an envoy actively attempting murder within the king’s court.”

Renn looked toward Lucien.

“This is political theater.”

Lucien finally stood.

“No.”

His voice carried through the chamber.

“This is my kingdom.”

Vesper Mana rolled through the floor.

Dark blue.

Controlled.

Ancient.

“And you tried to kill a witness beneath my roof.”

Renn went pale.

Lucien looked toward the guards.

“Take him.”

Renn stood abruptly.

“If you imprison a Northern Council representative, the grain compact ends.”

Lady Merrow flinched.

Several nobles exchanged looks.

There.

The real consequence.

Not a battle.

Trade.

Winter.

Families.

Politics.

Zee looked toward Rez.

This was his world now.

Not something Zee could punch into simplicity.

Rez looked toward his father.

Lucien did not answer for him.

Interesting.

Rez understood.

His decision.

The Crown Prince turned toward Renn.

“How many villages use that grain compact?”

Renn smiled.

“Enough.”

“How many Northern merchants depend on Vesper salt roads?”

Renn’s smile weakened.

“How many depend on our river wards?”

Silence.

Rez continued.

“How many northern healers buy Vesper frostroot?”

Renn stared.

Rez walked closer.

“You built your plan on frightened villages being too divided to resist you.”

“You are making the same mistake here.”

Renn’s jaw tightened.

Rez looked toward the Vesper nobles.

“If the Northern Council suspends grain passage, Vesper suspends reciprocal river protection and opens emergency trade through the western covens.”

Lady Merrow frowned.

“That will cost us.”

“Yes.”

Rez did not pretend otherwise.

“For a season.”

“Possibly two.”

Hane asked, “And if the Northern Council retaliates further?”

Rez looked toward Renn.

“Then we negotiate with people who did not kidnap children.”

Lucien smiled.

Small.

Proud.

Decision made.

The guards took Renn.

No one stopped them.

The political problem did not vanish.

It became real.

That was different.

By sunset, three affected villages had copies of Kest’s testimony.

The Border Council received formal notice that Maelor Renn was imprisoned for attempted murder under Vesper law.

Two northern councilors immediately denied knowledge of his scheme.

One demanded his release.

Another requested the evidence privately.

The grain compact was suspended before nightfall.

Vesper’s western trade office began opening alternate routes an hour later.

Nothing ended cleanly.

Permanent progress rarely did.

But Renn no longer controlled the children he had weaponized.

His mercenary oath was broken.

His scheme was public.

His protection had limits.

And Rez Vesper had made his first decision of the conflict knowing exactly what it might cost his kingdom.

⸻

Zee found Ner on the western tower after dark.

No guards.

No council.

No Aurelia.

Just wind.

Ner sat on the parapet with one knee raised, Pure Golden Mana turning slowly between his fingers.

Not working.

Playing.

A ribbon of light passed between his knuckles and became a tiny wolf.

Then a bird.

Then something with horns.

Zee frowned.

“Is that me?”

Ner looked at the Golden creature.

“It is supposed to be a goat.”

“That is worse.”

Ner smiled.

The light vanished.

Zee sat beside him.

Not too close.

Then closer than necessary anyway.

For several moments neither spoke.

Vesper stretched beneath them.

Winter fires.

Watchtowers.

Moving guards.

A kingdom accepting another political enemy because its king and prince had refused to hand children back to the people who profited from them.

Zee looked toward Ner’s hands.

“Did it hurt?”

“The oath?”

“Yes.”

“Not exactly.”

Zee waited.

Ner sighed.

“It felt like holding a door shut while somebody stronger kept kicking it.”

Zee’s jaw tightened.

“I should have—”

“No.”

Zee looked over.

Ner met his eyes.

“You did exactly what you were supposed to do.”

“I stood there.”

“Yes.”

“I hated it.”

“I know.”

“I could have helped.”

“You could have made it worse.”

That irritated him because it was true.

Ner continued.

“And when I actually needed you, you moved.”

Zee looked down.

Renn’s wrist breaking beneath his hand returned to him.

The scream.

The fear.

The smile Zee had felt on his own face.

“I liked it.”

Ner went quiet.

Zee had not intended to say that aloud.

Now that he had, he could not take it back.

“When I hurt him.”

Ner watched him.

Zee continued.

“I knew he deserved to be stopped.”

“That is not the same thing.”

“I know.”

He rubbed one thumb over a Crimson scale.

“I liked hearing him scream.”

Ner did not recoil.

That almost made it worse.

“What do you want me to say?”

“I do not know.”

“That you are secretly nice?”

Zee looked offended.

“I am nice.”

Ner stared.

“Sometimes.”

“Rude.”

A small smile passed between them.

Then disappeared.

Ner looked over Vesper.

“Did you want to kill him?”

Zee thought.

“Yes.”

“Did you?”

“No.”

“Why?”

Zee remembered Renn’s throat beneath his hand.

Aurelia’s voice.

Then Ner’s.

“Because you told me to stop.”

Ner looked back.

“That is not enough.”

Zee frowned.

“I stopped.”

“This time.”

That landed.

Ner was not judging him.

That made Zee listen.

“If the only reason you stop is because one of us says your name, what happens when we are not there?”

Zee stared at him.

Wind passed between them.

Somewhere far east, the Dragon Link carried a faint impression from Zareth.

Stone chambers.

Heat.

Drakonis.

Then concern.

Zee had been thinking hard enough for the Link to feel the weight of it.

He let the sensation pass without reaching for Zareth.

This answer belonged to him.

“I do not know.”

Ner nodded.

“Then figure it out before you become stronger.”

Zee almost laughed.

“That sounds threatening.”

“It is.”

“You are threatening me?”

“I am your best friend.”

“That is apparently worse.”

Ner smiled.

Zee looked at him.

Again too long.

Ner noticed.

Again.

This time he did not look away.

Neither did Zee.

The air shifted.

Not magic.

Something much more inconvenient.

Zee became aware that their knees were almost touching.

That Ner’s face was closer than he expected.

That his eyes were warmer in firelight than Pure Golden Mana ever looked.

Zee’s horns heated.

Ner glanced upward.

Then smiled.

Zee immediately looked toward the city.

“Do not.”

“I said nothing.”

“You were going to.”

“I was.”

Zee pushed his shoulder.

Ner pushed back.

Their hands hit the stone between them at the same time.

Fingers brushed.

Neither moved.

For one heartbeat.

Two.

Then footsteps sounded below.

Both pulled away far too quickly.

Rez emerged from the tower stair.

He looked at Zee.

Then Ner.

Then the suspicious distance suddenly placed between them.

His face became unbearable.

Zee pointed.

“No.”

Rez raised both hands.

“I came to tell you dinner is ready.”

Ner stood.

“Good.”

He walked past Rez.

Rez waited until Ner disappeared down the stairs.

Then looked at Zee.

Zee stood.

“Say one word.”

Rez smiled.

“I am proud of your commitment to diplomacy.”

Zee shoved him into the wall.

Rez laughed the entire way downstairs.

⸻

Aurelia was awake when Zee returned to his room.

Of course she was.

She sat beside the window holding a book she had not turned a page of in several minutes.

Zee stopped.

“Were you waiting?”

“Yes.”

“I am fifteen.”

“I noticed.”

“That means you do not have to wait every time.”

“I know.”

She closed the book.

“That does not mean I won’t.”

Zee walked over.

For a moment he considered sitting across from her.

Instead he sat beside her.

Then leaned against her shoulder.

Fifteen did not make that unnecessary.

Aurelia wrapped one arm around him.

Neither spoke at first.

Then:

“I hurt Renn.”

“I heard.”

“I liked it.”

“I know.”

Zee looked up.

“How?”

“You smiled.”

His stomach tightened.

Aurelia had seen.

She did not look frightened.

That mattered too much.

“Does that make me bad?”

Aurelia considered the question instead of answering quickly.

“No.”

Zee frowned.

“That was too easy.”

“I was not finished.”

Of course.

She touched one of the scales along his wrist.

“What you enjoy tells you something about yourself.”

“It does not make every choice for you.”

Zee looked toward the window.

“What if I enjoy it more?”

“Then you had better become very good at knowing why you are hurting someone.”

That answer surprised him.

No demand that he become gentle.

No lie that violence was always wrong.

Aurelia knew their world better than that.

Zee asked, “And if they deserve it?”

“People use that sentence to justify terrible things.”

His face tightened.

“Victor thought I deserved what he did.”

Aurelia went still.

There it was.

The line Zee feared crossing.

Victor had once looked at an infant and convinced himself fear was judgment.

That danger lived in certainty.

Aurelia spoke quietly.

“Then remember him.”

Zee looked at her.

“Not to become him.”

“To remember how easy it is to believe fear makes you righteous.”

Zee thought about Renn.

He had evidence.

Children.

Oaths.

A clear crime.

That had not made breaking the man’s wrist necessary.

The first break stopped his casting.

The second had been because Zee wanted the scream.

He knew the difference.

That was what frightened him.

And perhaps what could protect him too.

“I broke it twice.”

Aurelia understood.

“The first time?”

“To stop him.”

“The second?”

Zee looked down.

“Because I wanted to.”

No excuse.

No monster.

No prophecy.

Just truth.

Aurelia kissed his temple beside one horn.

“Then remember that difference.”

Zee closed his eyes.

Outside, Vesper prepared for a trade dispute.

Rez was probably still arguing with Lucien over alternate grain routes.

Ner was probably pretending the tower moment had not happened.

Zareth was several kingdoms away inside Drakonis, carrying responsibilities that belonged to him alone.

The core four were not children anymore.

Not adults.

Not rulers.

Not legends.

But the world had begun bringing them problems that could not be solved by surviving until morning.

At fifteen, Zee Darkheart was learning that choosing a hunt was easier than choosing what happened when he caught what he hunted.

He could break bones.

He could spill blood.

He could frighten people who deserved fear.

And somewhere beneath the part of him that protected innocents, another part had begun enjoying what monsters sounded like when they realized the monster in front of them was stronger.

Zee did not hate that part.

Not yet.

That was the danger.

He leaned against Aurelia and listened to her heartbeat while he still could.

Then opened his eyes.

Because if Zee Darkheart was going to decide what kind of monster the world deserved—

he would have to make sure the world did not decide it for him first.

[575] 2026-08-24 23:17 (4878 chars)
================================================================================
Create a code for this “YES. That’s the next real escalation. 🔥

We should stop treating 21 like a permanent present-day age. Zee is immortal in the sense that he cannot permanently die, but that does not mean the story freezes him at twenty-one. Time keeps moving. Zee keeps getting older. Zareth, Ner, and Rez keep getting older too. Twenty-one is just where we are right now.

So after Chapter 75, I’d start letting years move naturally again. Not necessarily giant jumps every chapter, but enough that the book feels like we are actually watching Zee’s life, not one year stretched across hundreds of chapters.

He can become 22.

And keep going.

His face/body can mature with age instead of being frozen forever. His supernatural nature can slow or alter aging later if we ever want that, but right now there is no reason to lock him at twenty-one.

And the bigger thing:

It is finally time for Zee to create hybrids.

Not because Zee wakes up and thinks:

“I wonder what my blood can make.”

That would be too random.

The world needs to force him into it.

The attacks keep coming.

Different kingdoms.

Different supernatural species.

Assassins.

Poisons.

Sieges.

Blood weapons.

Magic.

Creatures.

People attacking villages.

People attacking children.

People attacking Darkheart citizens specifically because they chose Zee.

At first Zee keeps saving them himself.

Then Darkheart soldiers get stronger.

Then his witches improve.

Then his Wolves.

Then Vampires.

But eventually Zee hits a situation where ordinary supernatural people simply cannot survive what is coming.

That is when the hybrid creation becomes emotional.

Imagine a major attack where Darkheart is being overwhelmed.

A Witch Zee cares about is dying.

A Vampire is dying beside them.

A Wolf is torn apart protecting civilians.

Zee can save people individually, but he cannot be everywhere.

And then Zee realizes something Ner already proved years earlier:

His blood can preserve one supernatural nature while adding another.

Ner was the accident.

Now Zee finally understands the implication.

That’s when he makes the choice deliberately.

Not:

Create an army.

At first:

Save this person.

That person wakes up changed.

And Zee realizes:

Oh.

😭🔥

That can become the birth of the first true hybrid race.

And I would NOT create all three kinds immediately.

Let them emerge through different crises.

For example, one attack could produce the first:

Witch–Vampire

Later a completely different event forces:

Vampire–Wolf

Then later:

Wolf–Witch

That makes each original hybrid creation matter historically.

And every one should begin with a person, not a race name.

Someone Zee knows.

Someone dying.

Someone he chooses to save.

Then the world eventually realizes:

Zee Darkheart can create new supernatural bloodlines.

THAT is when the endless attacks can get even worse. 😭

Because suddenly Zee isn’t only dangerous because of what he is.

Now kingdoms realize he can change what other people are.

And that creates a perfect vicious cycle:

The world attacks Zee because it fears him.

Zee needs stronger people to protect Darkheart.

Zee creates hybrids to keep his people alive.

The world sees the hybrids.

The world becomes even more afraid.

They attack harder.

Zee evolves.

His hybrids become stronger.

More people choose Darkheart because it becomes one of the few places where mixed supernatural people can survive.

Darkheart grows.

The world panics more.

Attack → adaptation → growth → fear → bigger attack → more adaptation.

That is basically the prophecy becoming a civilization-level problem now.

And this is where Zee can eventually stop feeling like only King of House Darkheart.

His kingdom starts becoming something fundamentally different under him.

Not because he announces:

“I will build a supernatural empire.”

It happens because people keep coming to him for protection.

Witches.

Vampires.

Wolves.

Mixed blood.

Outcasts.

People kingdoms want dead.

And Zee keeps saying:

Come here.

Then somebody attacks them.

And Zee kills the attacker. 😭

One thing I really want to protect, though: the hybrids shouldn’t instantly become disposable soldiers.

The first ones should feel historic and personal.

Zee is creating people, not weapons.

They can fight for him.

They can defend Darkheart.

They may swear loyalty.

But they should have personalities, families, wants, disagreements, and their own reasons for choosing Zee.

That fits Zee much better because he hates ownership.

So the next era of the story becomes:

Zee aging.

The endless attacks getting worse.

Darkheart expanding.

Zee realizing he cannot personally protect everyone.

The first deliberate hybrid creation.

Then the world discovering that the First Natural Tribrid can create supernatural bloodlines of his own.

Yeah.

That’s where we go now. 😌🩸🔥”
